NOTES
Note 1. Morphine: This heading refers to morphine in the following forms: (a) pure morphine:
(b) crude morphine; (c) salts of morphine; (d) preparations which contain more than 2o per cent of
morphine made direct from raw or medicinal opium; (e) preparations which contain more than o.2 per
cent of morphine made from any of the forms of morphine mentioned in (a), (b), (c) or (d); (f) solutions
and dilutions of morphine in an inert substance, liquid or solid, made from any of the forms of morphine
mentioned in (a), (b), (c) or (d), even if these solutions and dilutions contain o.2 per cent or less of morphine.
Where the figure entered in this column includes pure morphine contained in crude morphine, the
weight of such pure morphine and also the weight of the crude morphine should be indicated separately
under "Remarks".
Note 2. Cocaine: This heading refers to cocaine in the following forms: (a) pure cocaine; (b) salts
of cocaine; (c) preparations which contain more than o.1 per cent of cocaine made direct from the coca
leaf; (d) preparations which contain more than o.1 per cent of cocaine made from any of the forms of
cocaine mentioned in (a), (b) or (c); (e) solutions and dilutions of cocaine in an inert substance, liquid
or solid, made from any of the forms of cocaine mentioned in (a), (b) or (c), even if these solutions and
dilutions contain o.r per cent or less of cocaine.
Note 3. When figures are given for the substances mentioned below in answer to the questions in
the present form, the quantities for each drug should be inserted in the blank columns, additional columns
being added, if necessary:
Dihydromorphine and its salts (paramorfan) and preparations;
Morphine-N-oxide (genomorphine) and its preparations;
Thebaine and its salts and preparations;
Ecgonine and its salts and preparations;

The esters of morphine — except diacetyimorphine — and their salts and preparations;
Benzylmorphine and its salts (peronine) and preparations;
The other ethers of morphine and their salts and preparations, except methylmorphine (codeine)
and its salts and preparations and ethylmorphine and its salts (dionine) and preparations;
The esters of the following: dihydrohydroxycodeinone, dihydrocodeinone, dihydromorphinone,
acetyldihydrocodeinone or acetyldemethylodihydrothebaine, dihydromorphine and their
salts and preparations;
The morphine-N-oxide derivatives and the other pentavalent nitrogen morphine derivatives
and their preparations.
Note 4. The figures should include the quantities in bonded warehouses, free ports and free zones.
But, if a shipment in transit through the country, accompanied by a proper export authorisation or
diversion certificate (see Article 15 of the Geneva Convention), is placed for a time in a bonded ware-
house, free port or free zone, such shipment is not to be included in the stocks.
TABLE OF EQUIVALENCES
(By pure alkaloid is meant basic anhydrous alkaloid.)
Opium: One kilogramme of tincture is the equivalent of roo grammes of medicinal opium.
One kilogramme of extract is the equivalent of 2 kilogrammes of medicinal opium.
Indian hemp: One kilogramme of tincture is the equivalent of about roo grammes of Indian hemp.
One kilogramme of extract is the equivalent of about 7 kilogrammes of Indian hemp.
Morphine: The principal morphine salts found on the market contain about 8o per cent of pure morphine.
Diacetylmorphine (diamorphine, heroin): The principal diacetylmorphine salts (diamorphine, heroin)
found on the market contain about go per cent of pure diacetyimorphine.
Cocaine: Hydrochloride of cocaine contains about go per cent of pure cocaine.
Nitrate of cocaine contains 75 per cent of pure cocaine.
Tincture of coca ordinarily contains o.2 per cent of pure cocaine.
Fluid extract of coca ordinarily contains o.6 per cent of pure cocaine.
Dihydrohydroxycodeinone: Hydrochloride of dihydrohydroxycodeinone (eucodal) contains 78 per cent
of pure dihydrohydroxycodeinone.
Dihydrocodeinone: Bitartrate of dihydrocodeinone (dicodide) contains 6o per cent of pure dihydro-
codeinone.
Dihydromorphinone: Hydrochloride of dihydromorphinone (dilaudide) contains 89 per cent of pure
dihydromorphinone.
Acetyldihydrocodeinone or acetyldemethylodihydrothebaine: Hydrochloride of acetyldihydrocodeinone or
acetyldemethylodihydrothebaine (acedicone) contains go per cent of pure acetyldihydrocodeinone.
Dihydromorphine: Hydrochloride of dihydromorphine (paramorfan) contains 89 per cent of pure dihydro-
morphine.
Benzylmorphine: Hydrochloride of benzylmorphine (peronine) contains 87 per cent of pure benzyi-
morphine.
Methylmorphine (codeine): Phosphate of codeine contains on an average 7o per cent of pure methyl-
morphine (codeine).
Hydrochloride of codeine contains 8r per cent of pure methylmorphine (codeine).
Sulphate of codeine contains 76 per cent of pure methylmorphine (codeine).
Ethylmorphine: Hydrochloride of ethylmorphine (dionine) contains 81 per cent of pure ethylmorphine.

These statistics relate to the calendar year 19.
In this form the term " Geneva Convention " has been used to denote the Opium Convention signed
at Geneva on February 19th, 1925, and the term "Limitation Convention" to denote the Convention
for limiting the Manufacture and regulating the Distribution of Narcotic Drugs signed at Geneva on
July 13th, 1931.
The letters G and/or L placed at the head of a column or against an item indicate the Convention
in virtue of which the information is required — i.e.:
G = Information required in virtue of the Geneva Convention;
L = Information required in virtue of the Limitation Convention.
A Government Party to only one Convention need only supply information when the letter denoting
that particular Convention appears at the head of a column. The Board would, however, greatly
appreciate it if a Party to only one Convention could see its way to supplying the information required
under the other Convention also.
GENERAL INSTRUCTIONS
Show weight in kilogrammes; if impossible, state clearly the weight used in the table.
Quantities less than one kilogramme should be omitted from the figures entered in this report.
Fill in every blank space in every column. Where there is nothing to report, or less than one kilo-
gramme of weight, write the word 'nil".
If there is not sufficient space on this form, attach additional pages with a proper designation at
the head of each page.
Figures should be shown without full-stops or commas.
Only net weights should be entered in the table (i.e., excluding packing material, such as cases,
bottles, tubes and other containers, wrappers, etc.).
Urless otherwise indicated, only the weight of the pure alkaloid content should be given in the case of
crude alkaloids and of salts and preparations. (See table of equivalences at the end of this form.)
REFERENCES
Article 22, paragraph I (e), of the Geneva Convention.
Article 13 of the Limitation Convention.
For the definitions, see Article I of the Geneva and Limitation Conventions, and also Notes I and 2
on this form.

NOTES
Note 1. Morphine: This heading refers to morphine in the following forms: (a) pure morphin
(b) crude morphine; (c) salts of morphine; (d) preparations which contain more than ao per cent of
morphine made direct from raw or medicinal opium; (e) preparations which contain more than o.  per
cent of morphine made from any of the forms of morphine mentioned in (a), (b), (c) or (d); (f) olutiona
mentioned in (a), (b), (c) or (d), even if these solutions and dilutions contain o.2 per cent or less of morphine
Where the figure entered in this column includes pure morphine contained in crude morphine, the
weight of such pure morphine and also the weight of the crude morphine should be indicated separately
under "Remarks".
Note 2. Cocaine: This heading refers to cocaine in the following forms: (a) pure cocaine; (b) salts
of cocaine; (c) preparations which contain more than o.r per cent of cocaine made direct from the coca
leaf; (d) preparations which contain more than o.I per cent of cocaine made from any of the forms of
cocaine mentioned in (a), (b) or (c); (e) solutions and dilutions of cocaine in an inert substance, liquid
or solid, made from any of the forms of cocaine mentioned in (a), (b) or (c), even if these solutions and
dilutions contain o.1 per cent or less of cocaine.
Note 3. When figures are given for the substances mentioned below in answer to the questions
in the present form, the quantities for each drug should be inserted in the blank columns, additional
columns being added, if necessary:
Dihydromorphine and its salts (paramorfan) and preparations;
Morphine-N-oxide (genomorphine) and its preparations;
Thebaine and its salts and preparations;
Ecgonine and its salts and preparations;
The esters of ecgonine and their salts and preparations;
The esters of morphine — except diacetylmorphine — and their salts and preparations;
Benzylmorphine and its salts (peronine) and preparations;
The other ethers of morphine and their salts and preparations, except methylmorphine (codeine)
and its salts and preparations and ethylmorphine and its salts (dionine) and preparations;
The esters of the following: dihydrohydroxycodeinone, dihydrocodeinone, dihydromorphinone,
acetyldihydrocodeinone or acetyldemethylodihydrothebaine, dihydromorphine and their
salts and preparations;
The morphine-N-oxide derivatives and the other pentavalent nitrogen morphine derivatives
and their preparations.
TABLE OF EQUIVALENCES
(By pure alkaloid is meant basic anhydrous alkaloid.)
Opium: One kilogramme of tincture is the equivalent of roo grammes of medicinal opium.
One kilogramme of extract is the equivalent of 2 kilogrammes of medicinal opium.
Indian hemp: One kilogramme of tincture is the equivalent of about roo grammes of Indian hemp.
One kifogramme of extract is the equivalent of about 7 kilogranmmes of Indian hemp.
Morphine: The principal morphine salts found on the market contain about 8o per cent of pure morphine.
Diacetylmorphine (diamorphine, heroin): The principal diacetylmorphine salts (diamorphine, heroin)
found on the market contain about go per cent of pure diacetyimorphine.
Cocaine: Hydrochloride of cocaine contains about go per cent of pure cocaine.
Nitrate of cocaine contains 75 per cent of pure cocaine.
Tincture of coca ordinarily contains o.2 per cent of pure cocaine.
Fluid extract of coca ordinarily contains o.6 per cent of pure cocaine.
Dihydrohydroxycodeinone: Hydrochloride of dihydrohydroxycodeinone (cucqdal) contains 78 per cent
of pure dihydrohydroxycodeinone.
Dihydrocodeinone: Bitartrate of dihydrocodeinone (dicodide) contains 6o per cent of pure dihydro-
codeinone.
Dihydromorphinone: Hydrochloride of dihydromorphinone (dilaudide) contains 89 per cent of pure
dihydromorphinone.
Acetyldihydrocodeinone or acetyldemethylodihydrothebaine: Hydrochloride of acetyldihydrocodeinone or
acetyldemethylodihydrothebaine (acedicone) contains 9o per cent of pure acetyldihydrocodeinone.
Dihydromorphine: Hydrochloride of dihydromorphine (paramorfan) contains 89 per cent of pure
dihydromorphine.
Benzylmorphine: Hydrochloride of benzylmorphine (peronine) contains 87 per cent of pure benzyl-
morphine.
Methylmorphine (codeine): Phosphate of codeine contains on an average 7o per cent of pure methyl-
morphine (codeine).
Hydrochloride of codeine contains 81 per cent of pure methyimorphine (codeine).
Sulphate of codeine contains 76 per cent of pure methylmorphine (codeine).
Ethylmorphine: Hydrochloride of ethylmorphine (dionine) contains 81 per cent of pure ethylmorphine.

=== Page 254 === (confidence 0.99)
Note by the Secretary-General
In accordance with Article 2l of the Convention for
limiting the Manufacture and regulating the Distribution
o f Narcotic Drugs of 13 July 193l, as amended by the
Protocol of 11 December 1946, the Secretary-General has
the honour to communicate the following report to the
Members of the United Nations and to the other parties to
the Convention.
(For the form of annual reports, see document E/NR.1948/Form.)

=== Page 255 === (confidence 0.00)


=== Page 256 === (confidence 1.00)
Original: English
A. GENERAL
I. LAWS AND PUBLICATIONS
1. Titles of Laws and Regulations:
a. Laws:
1) Narcotic Control Law (Law No. 123, 10 July 1948).
2) Marihuana Control Law (Law No. 124, 10 July 1948).
3) The Law for Partial Amendment of the Narcotic Control Law (Law No. 238,
10 December 1948 - effective 1 January 1949).
b. Regulations:
1) Enforcement Regulations of Narcotic Control Law (Ministry of Welfare
Ordinance No. 26, 22 July 1948).
2) Enforcement Regulations of Marihuana Control Law (Ministries of Welfare
and Agriculture and Forestry Ordinance No. 1, 22 July 1948).
2. The Narcotic Control Regulations promulgated 19 June 1946 and the Marihuana
Control Regulations promulgated 23 April 1947 were rescinded and the Laws as
listed above became effective through Diet action. Provisions of these two
Laws are summarized as follows:
a. The Narcotic Control Law (Law No. 123 of 1948) as amended requires that
narcotic dealers obtain licenses from the Minister of Welfare. Narcotic
dealers are classified as Importers, Manufacturers, Compounders, Producers,
Central Wholesale Dealers, Local Wholesale Dealers, Retail Dealers, Practi-
tioners, Administrators, Dealers in Exempt Preparations, Narcotic Research
Workers. Licenses issued to the narcotic dealers are effective from the
date of issue to 3l December of the same year.
The Law provides that no person shall commit the following acts:
1) Cultivation of plants which produce narcotics.
2) Export of narcotics.
3)
Possession, importation, manufacture, compounding, producing, admi-
mistering, dispensing, prescribing, selling, buying, giving away or
receiving diacetylmorphine, its salts, compounds, or preparations
thereof.
4)
Becoming addicted to narcotics so as to be a menace to the public
welfare because of addiction or to lose self control as a result of
narcotic addiction.
The Law has the following provisions regarding narcotic dealers:
1) Narcotic dealers are required to maintain a permanent record, for
each business office, in which must be entered, with identifying
information, the name and quantity of the narcotics imported, manu-
factured, compounded, produced, received, sold, purchased, admini-
stered, dispensed, or otherwise distributed or used for research.
2)
When a narcotic dealer sells, gives away, purchases or receives
narcotics, other than exempt narcotic preparations, from or to
another narcotic dealer, he must deliver a transfer form or receipt
form issued by the government to the other party by entering all
necessary information and placing his name and seal on the form.
The Law requires any importer, manufacturer, compounder, producer,
central wholesale dealer or local wholesale dealer to submit a monthly
report to the Minister of Welfare on the name and quantity of narcotics on
hand at the beginning of the month, the name and quantity of narcotics
imported, manufactured, compounded, produced, bought or sold during the
month, including to whom or from whom, and the name and quantity of nar-
cotics on hand at the end of the month.
A narcotic importer is required, before importing narcotics, to receive
authorization from the Minister of Welfare in regard to the name and quan-
tity of narcotics to be imported and other information provided for by
Ministerial Ordinance.
A narcotic manufacturer, a narcotic compounder or a narcotic producer
is required to obtain authorization from the Minister of Welfare for every
5

=== Page 257 === (confidence 0.99)
quarter of the year for; the specific quantity of each narcotic to be manu-
factured, compounded or produced, the amount of each narcotic to be used
for such purpose; and is required to submit a report to the Minister of
Welfare on the results at the end of each quarter.
In order to obtain the annual renewal of license, a narcotic retail
dealer, a narcotic practitioner, a narcotic administrator or a narcotic
research worker is required to submit a report to the Minister of Wel fare
showing; the quantity of each narcotic on hand on the date of application
for the previous license; the name and quantity of narcotics purchased,
sold, administered, dispensed or used in research between the date of appli-
cation for the previous license and that of application for renewal; and
the quantity of each narcotic on hand on the date of application for the
renewal of license.
In each of the reports mentioned above, the name, address and the
registration number of the narcotic dealer must be entered.
Maximum penalties provided by the Law are five years penal servitude
or a fine not exceeding ¥50,000 (USA $175), or both. Any attempted viola-
tion is also subject to punishment.
b.
The Marihuana Control Law (Law No. 124 of 1948) prescribes that any person
who wishes to be a marihuana dealer must be licensed by the Minister of
Welfare.
In this Law, the term "marihuana dealer" means a marihuana producer or
a marihuana research worker. A marihuana producer is a person who culti-
vates marihuana plants for the purpose of producing hemp fiber or seeds.
A marihuana research worked is a person who cultivates marihuana plants for
the purpose of fiber research.
No person shall commit the following acts:
1) Export or import of marihuana.
2) Possession, sale, administering, dispensing, or other distribution
of medicines which are manufactured or compounded from marihuana
plants.
Each marihuana producer or marihuana research worker is required to
maintain a record, and submit a quarterly report to the Minister of Wel-
fare on; the area of plots or fields under cultivation; the quantity of mari-
huana plants and seeds on hand; the quantity of marihuana purchased or sold;
the dates of purchase or sale; the name of the persons from whom or to
whom marihuana was purchased or sold, including the name, address and
registration number.
C.
When the Law for Amendment of the Code of Criminal Procedure (Law No. 131
of 1948) became effective 1 January 1949, and Law No. 112, 27 September 1947,
therefore became ineffective, the Law for Partial Amendment to the Narcotic
Control Law (Law No. 238 of 1948) amending Article 53 of the Law, passed by
the Diet 10 December 1948 with 1 January 1949 as the effective date, became
effective thereby strengthening the control of narcotics by; adding pro-
visions for offenses concerning marihuana to those concerning narcotics;
giving narcotic control officers judicial power of arrest and designating
them as narcotic agents (Mayaku Torishimari-In); increasing the number of
narcotic agents from the previous 200 to 250; and authorizing the agents
to carry small arms.
d.
The Enforcement Regulations of the Narcotic Control Law (Ministry of Wel-
fare Ordinance No. 26 of 1948) prescribe regulations necessary for the
enforcement of the Law.
Among other provisions, demerol and amidon are specifically designated
as narcotics under the authority vested in the Minister of Welfare.
The Enforcement Regulations of the Marihuana Control Law (Ministries of
Welfare and Agriculture and Forestry Ordinance No. 1 of 1948) prescribe
regulations necessary for the enforcement of the law.
3. There are no important publications to report.
II. ADMINISTRATION
1. Administrative Arrangements for the Execution of the International Conventions:

=== Page 258 === (confidence 0.99)
a. The personnel of the Narcotic Section of the Ministry of Welfare was
increased to 13 officials and ten clerks.
b.
Duties of the Narcotic Section, Ministry of Welfare are:
1) To maintain a register of narcotic dealers and marihuana dealers
showing the name, address, registration number, date of registration,
and classification.
2)
To be responsible for the appointment, discharge, direction and super-
vision of narcotic agents who are vested with judicial power of arrest
whose duties include:
a) Enforcing the provisions of the Narcotic Control Law and the Mari-
huana Control Law.
b)
Performing the functions of judicial police under the provisions of
Criminal Procedure Law (Law No. 131 of 1948) concerning crimes
involving narcotics or marihuana under the Narcotic Control Law and
the Marihuana Control Law, and crimes involving prepared opium
under Chapter 14 of the Criminal Code.
c)
Conducting investigations concerning violations of the above laws.
During 1948 inspections were made of 21,216 narcotic dealers, and
2,830 investigations were originated, of which 1,746 cases were
established as offenses, including 1,070 cases resulting in court
action. The other 676 offenses were minor violations and were
disposed of administratively.
3) To be responsible for the proper enforcement of the above laws by
directing and supervising the following:
a) Narcotic agents - 151 as of 31 December 1948.
b) Administrative officials - 377 as of 31 December 1948.
c) Clerks - 137 as of 31 December 1948.
c. No important difficulties were encountered in the application of any of the
Conventions either generally or in respect of particular cases.
2. The narcotic agents, by the end of 1948, had obtained information on 5,076
addicts. As described in the 1947 report, the Narcotic Section of the Ministry
of Welfare has authority to control narcotics, and under the supervision and
direction of this Section the narcotic agents were active in the control of
narcotics and in the suppression of illicit traffic throughout the country.
3.
All of the narcotic agents have been trained in methods of investigation of
criminal cases concerned with narcotics and have received specialized instruc-
tion in enforcement through the co-operation of the Attorney General's Office,
and the Ministry of Wel fare, with the Narcotic Control Division, Public Health
and Wel fare Section, General Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers. Schools held on three occasions, were attended by all the narcotic
agents. Such schools are now required as an integral part of a narcotic agent's
training.
III. CONTROL OF INTERNATIONAL TRADE
1.
As stated above the export of narcotics is prohibited.
2.
Import of narcotics was permitted only as authorized by the Supreme Commander
for the Allied Powers. No one was granted a license as a narcotic importer.
Codeine phosphate and morphine hydrochloride, requisitioned before stocks and
rate of consumption were accurately determined, were received from the United
States. Opium and crude morphine were transferred from Korea, as a security
measure, and were placed in secure storage after being accurately weighed and
analyzed.
3。
There were no diversions during the transmit of the above narcotics.

(
5. (
6. (There is nothing to report.

8. (

No international treaties or agreements on narcotic drugs were completed during

JAPANESE GOVERNMENT

TO:
GENERAL HEADQUARTERS OF THE SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS

SUBJECT: Agreement in Principle of the Japanese Government with the United Nations
Protocol on Narcotics Opened for Signature 11 December 1946 at Lake
Success, New York.
C.L.C.O. No. 3201(10)
10 September 1948
1. Reference is made to Memorandum from Public Health and Welfare Section,
GHQ, SCAP, PHMJG-56, dated 19 January 1948, for Narcotic Section, Ministry of Wel-
fare, Japanese Government, subject: Information Regarding Protocol of 1l December
1946 - United Nations.
2. The Japanese Government, considering that under the international Agree-
ments, Conventions and Protocols relating to Narcotic Drugs which were concluded on
23 January 1912, 11 February 1925, 19 February 1925, 13 July 1931, 27 November 1931
and 26 June 1936, the League of Nations was invested with certain duties and
functions for whose continued performance it is necessary to make provision in
consequence of the dissolution of the League, considering that it is expedient
that these duties and functions should be performed by the United Nations and the
World Health Organization, and considering that the Government has no authority to
deposit an instrument of acceptance with the Secretary-General of the United Nations,
wishes to express its agreement in principle with the provisions of the protocol
on Narcotic Drugs opened for signature 11 December 1946 at Lake Success, New York,
to the General Headquarters of the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers.
FOR THE DIRECTOR-GENERAL:
Director of First Division,
Central Liaison and
Coordination Office.
V.
ILLICIT TRAFFIC
1. Former Japanese Army and Navy narcotic stocks unreported under the Narcotic Law
continued to be a source of supply for illicit traffic during 1948. Registrants
arrested, totalling three-tenths of one per cent of all registrants, were in
many cases in illegal possession of such stocks. Toward the end of the year
evidence was obtained that foreign nationals were smuggling narcotics into
Japan from the Asiatic continent. These narcotics were in crude form but
indications were that finished narcotics have also reached Japan through
illicit channels from the same source.
In August, with the arrest of a foreign national narcotic trafficker, evi-
dence was obtained that heroin was being smoked in Japan either by mixing the
narcotics with tobacco or by placing a small amount of heroin on the end of
the cigarette. In November, a raid in Tokyo resulted in the arrest of twelve
persons ten of whom were addicts, including one foreign national who was the
source of supply for the group.
Portions of the heroin seized in the illicit traffic were of a high degree
purity, while other portions were adulterated with various substances princi-
pally magnesium carbonate. Some of the heroin was of a gray or yellowish color.
During 1947 and 1948 a total of ninety-five foreign nationals were arrested
for trafficking in narcotics. In 1949 by the middle of February a total of
fifty-one foreign nationals were arrested for narcotic violations which indi-
cates the increased importance of this class of trafficker with whom many
Japanese are usually involved. Reports show a large percentage of the addicts
apprehended in traffic sponsored by these foreign nationals are smoking heroin
rather than using injections.
Small amounts of narcotics were seized from repatriates arriving in Japan
8

=== Page 260 === (confidence 0.99)
included in which were 247.5 grammes of raw opium. Three seizures were made of
small amounts of narcotics intended for shipment out of Japan. Two defendants
were arrested and sentenced to six months penal servitude and one year suspended
sentence, respectively. Two persons escaped arrest.
Thefts from registrants alsc continued to be a source of supply for illicit
traffic. The number of thefts was reduced from a monthly average of thirty-
seven in 1947 to twenty-five in 1948. No wholesalers were burglarized. The
number of hospitals losing narcotics in this manner reached a peak of seven
during July but was reduced to one per month in October, November and December.
2.
Out of twelve persons arrested for cultivation of the upium poppy three were
admonished, three were fined and two were sentenced to penal servitude of ten
and six months. The number of plants cultivated was comparatively small, the
greatest seizure being 300 plants.
Seventy-two persons were admonished and l49 were arrested for illegal culti-
vation of marihuana in a total area of only five tan (1.25 acres). Thirty-four
persons were brought to trial for violation of the Marihuana Law with the
following results: Twenty-four were fined amounts varying from two to ten
thousand yen (USA $7 - $35); nine were sentenced to penal servitude for terms
varying from two to four months and one received a suspended sentence. None of
them were convicted of introducing the marihuana into illegal traffic.
A considerable amount of marihuana was discovered growing wild in the
northern island of Hokkaido. Measures are being taken to destroy such crops
during 1949. Information aids are being employed to apprise the populace of
the law prohibiting the cultivation of marihuana except by registered persons
and then for fiber purposes only.
3.
Twenty-one thousand two hundred sixteen registrants were inspected and 2,830
investigations were originated. There were 1,070 arrests for narcotic viola-
tions during the year. Of these 275 were registrants and 795 non-registrants.
Five hundred sixteen defendants were convicted for narcotic violations which
are classified as follows:
Illicit manufacture
1
Illegal possession
215
Illegal sale
205
Possession of opium poppy
5
Theft of narcotics
13
Forging narcotic documents
2
Others
75
Among the above persons ll5 were registrants, thirty of whom received sentences
of penal servitude totalling twenty-five years. three months. Seventy-two
registrants, including ten of those sentenced to penal servitude, were fined
a total of Y231,650 (USA $810.77). Twenty-three other registrants received
suspended sentences totalling sixty-nine years and were fined a total of
¥34,000 (USA $119).
Of the 401 non-registrants convicted, 132 were sentenced to penal servitude
for a total of eighty-four years ten months; 218, including fifty-three sentenced
to penal servitude, were fined a total of Y644,900 (USA $2,257.14) and 104
defendants received suspended sentences totalling 280 years and were fined a
total of ¥232,250 (USA $812.87).
4.

Remarks: About 20% of the narcotics found in illicit trafficking are adultera-

VI. OTHER INFORMATION
There is no additional information to report.
B. RAW MATERIALS
VII. RAW OPIUM
The cultivation, planting or possession of the opium poppy is prohibited in
Japan.
VIII. COCA LEAF
The cultivation, planting or possession of narcotic seeds or plants except
Cannabis Sativa L. (for fiber purposes only) is prohibited.
IX.
INDIAN HEMP
1.
Marihuana grows wild in certain limited areas of Hokkaido, and measures are
being taken to eradicate this marihuana.
2.
The area cultivated for fiber and seed totaled 3,893 hectares, of which 3,518
hectares were for fiber and 375 hectares were for seed.
3.
Illegal cultivation of marihuana totaled 0.5 hectares (1.25 acres). Marihuana
plants confiscated and destroyed totaled 6,658.
4.
Possession, sale, purchase or use of marihuana or marihuana preparations is
prohibited.
C. MANUFACTURED DRUGS
X.
INTERNAL CONTROL OF MANUFACTURED DRUGS
1. Explanation of control.
a. The manufacture of narcotics which has been prohibited since October 1945
was resumed, as an interim measure, for the medicinal needs of the Japanese
13

=== Page 265 === (confidence 0.98)
people. Specific amounts to !x: manufactured quarterly require the authori-
zation of the Minister of Wel fare, and monthly reports submitted by the
manufacturers are carefully checked and verified. The quantities of drugs
manufactured are not allowed to exceed the estimate.
b.
The possession, importation, menufacture, compounding, producing, adminis-
tering, dispensing, prescribing, selling, buying, giving away or receiving
diacetylmorphine, its salts, compounds or preparations thereof is prohibited
under the Law which provides a maximum penalty of five years penal servitude
and ¥50,000 (USA $175) fine or both.
2. Licenses
a. Four manufacturers were licensed to provide narcotics for domestic needs.
b. The Narcotic Control Law continued in effect the provisions regarding
licensing reported for 1947. Importers, manufacturers and administrators
were added as new classifications. No license was issued to import nar-
cotics. The term "administrator" as used in the Law means a person licensed
by the Minister of Wel fare to be responsible to receive and hold narcotics
to be used in the hospital or dispensary.
The number of licenses issued by the Minister of Welfare for narcotic
dealers is shown below:
Number of narcotic dealers
92,983
Number of marihuana dealers
25,506
3. Manufacture
a. The following factories, which were approved to complete equipment for
manufacturing narcotics in 1947, were licensed under the Narcotic Law,
and manufactured the products as shown below:

b. Demerol and amidon are both classified as narcotics under the Narcotic Law
and Regulations. There is no amidon in Japan, and no synthetic narcotics
of any kind were manufactured during 1948.
C.
Control over Factories
1) The narcotic manufacturers are under the strict supervision of the
Narcotic Section, Ministry of Welfare, and of the Narcotics Control
Division, Public Health and Welfare Section, General Headquarters,
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers.
2)
The factories and records are inspected and checked each month and at
the end of each quarter. Manufacturing results are examined and checked
against the assay of raw material used. The destruction of manufacturing
residues must be witnessed by a narcotic agent.
3)
Sales by factories are only for domestic use, and can be made only to
licensed compounders and producers.
4)
Quarterly reports are checked and verified by comparison of amount of
narcotics manufactured against assayed content, by determining stock on
hand, and by checking sales against receipts by compounders and pro-
ducers.
5)
Raw materials are analyzed in the laboratories of factories and in the
Tokyo Hygienic Laboratory of che Ainistry of Welfare. The customs ser-
vice is not authorized to conduct an analysis of raw narcotic materials.
14

b. The system of supervision over narcotic dealers is the same as that deseribed
in the 1947 report.* Inspections of registrants averaged 1,800 per month.
5. Manufacture and trade in diacetylmorphine
Possession, use or dealing in diacetylmorphine is punishable with five
years penal servitude or ¥50,000 (USA $175) fine or both.
D. OTHER QUESTIONS
XI. CHAPTER IV OF THE HAGUE OPIUM CONVENTION OF 1912
There is nothing to report concerning Chapter IV of the Hague Convention.
XII. PREPARED OPIUM
Possession, use, or sale of prepared opium is prohibited. There were no
seizures of prepared opium and no opium smokers were apprehended.
XIII. MISCELLANEOUS
The smoking of heroin either by mixing the drug with tobacco or by dipping the
cigarette into the drug was discovered among foreign nationals and their Japanese
associates. Vigorous enforcement efforts resulted in large scale arrests and
seizures during the first four months of 1949.

Note by the Secretary-General
In accordance with Article 2l of the Convention of 1931
for limiting the Manufacture and regulating the Distribution
of Narcotic Drugs, as amended by the Protocol of ll December
1946, the Secretary-General has the honour to forward to the
Members of the United Nations and to the other parties to the
Convention the following text communicated by the Government of
the United States of America.

Original: English
LAW NO. 238
LAW FOR PARTIAL AMENDMENT TO THE NARCOTIC CONTROL LAW
A Part of the Narcotic Control Law (Law No. 123 of 1948) shall be amended as fol-
lows:
The following Article shall be added next to Article 52.
Article 52 (2). The Minister of Wel fare shall designate Narcotic Agents (Mayaku-
torishimari-in) from among the Narcotic Control Officers (Mayaku-tosei-shuji) of
Metropolitan, Hokkaido and each prefecture, within the limit of 250 persons in
total.
(2) Under the direction and supervision of the Minister of Wel fare, Narcotic Agents
shall take charge of entering, inspecting and removing as authorized by this Law as
well as the Taima Control Law (Law No. 124 of 1948) and other matters concerning
the enforcement of these Laws, and he authorized, as judicial police officers pre-
scribed in the Code of Criminal Procedure, to perform their duties in respect to
the offences concerning narcotic or taima and also the offences rescribed in Chap-
ter 14 of the Penal Code (Law No. 45 of 1907).
(3) Narcotic Agents may perform their duties outside the area to their prefecture.
(4) Narcotic Agents shall be authorized to carry with them small arms in the exer-
cise of their duties.
In Article 53, "Narcotic Control Officers" (Mayaku-tosei-shuji) shall read "Narcotic
Agents" (Mayaku-torishimari-in),
Supplementary Provision:
The present Law shall come into force as from the day of enforcement (January
1, 1949) of the Law for Amendments to the Code of Criminal Procedure (Law No. 131
of 1948).
Attorney-General
Minister of Welfare
Prime Minister

Subject:
Narcotics in Japan Restituted to China.
FE
OFD
The Acting Political Adviser has the honor to refer to item
(3) of this Mission's airgram no. 215, September 1, 1949, and item
(1) of A-243, September 22, 1949, regarding narcotics in Japan
114 NARCOTI CS/9-2749
restituted to the Chinese National Government by General Headquarters,
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers.
scm
Information regarding these narcotics was supplied on September
24, 1949, upon request to an officer of this Mission by Mr. Ely M.
BRANDES, Chief of the Raw Materials Unit, Property Liquidation
006524
Division, Office of Civil Property Custodian, General Headquarters.
The statements made by Mr. Brandes confirm, in large part, the in-
formation previously reported, and indicate that no concern need
be felt by the Department over possible illicit sale of these nar-
cotics by the Chinese Mission in Japan.
According to Mr. Brandes, the Chinese Mission, in the name of
the Chinese National Government, on January 21, 1949, submitted to
the Office of Civil Property Custodian a claim for restitution of
all opium and opium derivatives now in Japan and identifiable as
having been removed from Chine during the war. Asked to supply the
necessary data regarding these narcotics, the Japanese Government
reported to the Office of Civil Property Custodian that a total of
21,146. 418 kilograms of opium and 214. 620 kilograms of crude morphine,
located at the premises of three Japanese pharmaceutical plants, had
been identified as having been removed from China during the war.
The Japanese Government reported further thet, of the totals
mentioned, approximately 3,600 kilograms had opium tax notes attached
which showed Manchuria as the country of origin. As to 1, O36 kilo-
grams of opium which was originally reported as former Japanese Navy
property, the Japanese Government stated that all opium formerly
held by that Navy had come from Manchuria, 'ongolia or Hainan Island.
The remainder of the opium, and the crude morphine, was reported by
the Japanese Government, on the basis of available records, as having
originated in China. The narcotics in question were reported by the
Japanese Government as being located at the premises of the Dai Nippon
Pharmaceutical Company Plant, Osaka; the Sankyo Company, Shinagawa
M
Plant, Tokyo, and the Takeda Pharmaceutical Company, Tokyo.
